=== lyme-acute-neuro-disease [local-clinical, partial] Acute neurological disease associated with lyme disease
  -- Inpatient: ORZID2 NE GMENU LYME ACUTE NEURO DISEASE (matched by name)
     [both delete clinical]
       - Neurological disease is a common manifestation of Lyme Disease that typically
       - occurs within weeks to months after the tick bite. Unilateral or bilateral
       - facial nerve palsy (Bell's palsy) can be treated with oral antimicrobials. Other
       - neurological manifestations, including meningitis, require treatment with
       - intravenous antimicrobials.
     [both delete clinical]
       - Duration of therapy
       - Treat for at least 14 days. Longer durations, up to 21 days for facial nerve
       - palsy and 28 days for other neurologic disease, may be required in severe cases.
     [one replace clinical]
       - _______________________________________________________________________________
       - References:
       - Halperin et. al., Neurology (2007) 69.91-102
       - Lantos et al., Clinical Infectious Diseases, 2021, 72(1):e1-e48
       - Mandell Chapter: Lyme Disease (Lyme Borreliosis) Due to Borrelia burgdorferi
       - Sanchez et al., JAMA (2016) 315.16
       - Sanford keyword: Lyme Disease, Facial Nerve Paralysis
       - Sanford keyword: Lyme Disease, Meningitis, Encephalitis
       - Up-to-date article: Nervous system Lyme disease
       + Duration of therapy
       + Treat for at least 14 days. Longer durations, up to 21 days for facial nerve
       + palsy and 28 days for other neurologic disease, may be required in severe cases.

=== lyme-disease-prophylaxis [local-clinical, auto] Lyme disease prophylaxis
  -- Inpatient: ORZID2 NE GMENU LYME DISEASE PROPHYLAXIS (matched by name)

     [both replace clinical shared:636A6-DesMoines,656-StCloud]
       - 5. Bite occurs in a highly endemic area.
       - CDC Lyme Disease Case Map
       + 5. Patient was exposed to tick bite in endemic areas where rate of Ixodes
       + scapularis infection with Borrelia burgdorferi is greater than 20%. Minnesota
       + counties where there is high risk of tickborne disease are shown in the
       + following link:
       + Tickborne Disease Risk in Minnesota
       + Providers can call the Minnesota Department of Health (651-201-5414) or the
       + Wisconsin Department of Health Services (608-266-1865) for specific rates of B.
       + burdorferi infection of Ixodes spp. ticks in area where a patient was exposed.
       + Contact Infectious Disease if patient was exposed to an Ixodes spp. tick bite in
       + New England or Mid-Atlantic States.

=== lyme-erythema-migrans [local-clinical, partial] Erythema migrans associated with lyme disease
  -- Inpatient: ORZID2 NE GMENU LYME ERYTHEMA MIGRANS (matched by name)
     [both insert clinical]
       + Treatment
       + Preferred:
       + Doxycycline 100 mg PO q12h ($) [DI] for 10 days
       + For pregnant patients or those with contraindications to doxycycline:
       + Amoxicillin 500 mg PO q8h ($) [R] for 14 days
     [both delete clinical]
       - If suspected erythema migrans is atypical, serology testing is recommended
       - with repeat in three weeks if negative.
       - Lyme Panel, Serum
       - Treatment
       - Preferred:
       - Doxycycline 100 mg PO q12h ($) [DI] for 10 days
       - For pregnant patients or those with contraindications to doxycycline:
       - Amoxicillin 500 mg PO q8h ($) [R] for 14 days
  -- Outpatient: ORZID3 NE GMENU LYME ERYTHEMA MIGRANS (matched by name)
     [both delete clinical]
       - Erythema migrans is the first manifestation of Lyme disease in 70-80% of cases.
       - It is typically an erythematous circular rash, 5cm or larger in diameter, with
       - induration and central clearing (although other patterns occur). It usually
       - develops at the site of the bite and/or in one or more distant locations. The
       - rash occurs 3 to 30 days after the bite. Rashes with onset less than 3 days
       - after a tick bite are not from Lyme disease.
       - If suspected erythema migrans is atypical, serology testing is recommended
       - with repeat in three weeks if negative.
       - Lyme Panel, Serum
     [one insert clinical]
       + Erythema migrans is the first manifestation of Lyme disease in 70-80% of cases.
       + It is typically an erythematous circular rash, 5cm or larger in diameter, with
       + induration and central clearing (although other patterns occur). It usually
       + develops at the site of the bite and/or in one or more distant locations. The
       + rash occurs 3 to 30 days after the bite. Rashes with onset less than 3 days
       + after a tick bite are not from Lyme disease.

       + PATIENTS UNDERGOING PROCEDURES THAT INVOLVE INFECTED SKIN,
       + SKIN STRUCTURES, OR INFECTED MUSCULOSKELETAL TISSUE
       + A patient with an infection involving skin, skin structures, or infected
       + musculoskeletal tissue may need antimicrobial for treatment of this infection
       + (links provided below). If so, the antimicrobial may also reduce the risk of
       + endocarditis in a person with a high risk cardiac lesion. Under these
       + circumstances, ensure that antimicrobial concentrations in tissues are
       + therapeutic for the duration of the procedure. If the patient is not
       + receiving systemic antimicrobial therapy for treatment of the infection,
       + consider giving one dose of an antimicrobial listed below for prevention of
       + endocarditis in a person with a high risk cardiac lesion.
       + Systemic treatment for infected skin, soft tissue, muscle bone or joint
       + Soft Tissue, Muscle, Bone and Joint
       + Skin Infection
       + Single dose prophylaxis in a person with a high risk cardiac lesion

       - malaise, myalgia, pelvic or perianal pain. On exam, the prostate gland is tense,

       - culture is positive. Men with dysuria and fever but without pyuria and/or
       + Acute bacterial prostatitis (ABP) typically presents with fever, dysuria,
       + chills, malaise, myalgia, pelvic or perianal pain. On exam, the prostate gland
       + is tense, boggy, and extremely tender. The urinalysis reveals pyuria, and the
       + urine culture is positive. Men with dysuria and fever but without pyuria and/or
     [both replace clinical]
       - especially in men older than 40 years, the patient should be seen by a urologist
       - and transurethral prostate resection for drainage should be considered.
       - Most cases in men aged 35 and older, those with recent urinary tract
       - instrumentation or surgery (e.g., prostate biopsy, vasectomy) are associated
       - with enteric Gram-negative rods. Most cases in men under age 35 and those who
       - are at high risk of sexually transmitted infections are associated with N.
       - gonorrhoeae and C. trachomatis. Resistant organisms are more likely if the
       - patient has been in the hospital for more than 48 hours or received a course of
       - antibiotics. There is increasing resistance in urinary pathogens to
       - ciprofloxacin.
       - A urine Gram stain and culture should be performed in all patients to guide
       - antimicrobial therapy:
       + the patient should be seen by a urologist.

       + organisms are more likely if the patient has been in the hospital for more than
       + 48 hours or received a course of antibiotics. There is increasing resistance in
       + urinary pathogens to fluroquinolones.
       + A midstream urine sample before antibiotics are taken and send for culture and
       + susceptibility testing should be performed in all patients to guide
       + antimicrobial therapy. When S. aureus is recovered from a urine
       + culture, it is important to perform blood cultures to evaluate whether the
       + bacteriuria reflects seeding of the prostate or urine from bacteremia.

=== chron-pros-pelvic-pain-syn [local-clinical, partial] CHRON PROS/PELVIC PAIN SYN
  -- Inpatient: ORZID2 NE GMENU ABX CHRON PROS/PELVIC PAIN SYN (matched by name)
     [both insert clinical]
       + Chronic prostatitis/chronic pelvic pain syndrome (CP/CPPS) is a clinical
       + syndrome in males defined by pain or discomfort in the pelvic region, often
       + accompanied by urologic symptoms or sexual dysfunction. Despite the use of the
       + term "prostatitis," it is unclear to what degree the prostate is the source of
       + symptoms.
       + Research guidelines define chronic prostatitis/chronic pelvic pain syndrome
       + (CP/CPPS) as chronic pelvic pain for at least three of the preceding six months
       + in the absence of other identifiable causes, often associated with urinary
       + symptoms and/or sexual dysfunction.
     [both replace]

       - alpha-adrenergic antagonists, anti-inflammatory medications) to reduce symptoms.

       + with CP/CPPS, antimicrobial therapy is not indicated.

=== chronic-bact-prostatitis [local-clinical, fallback] Chronic bacterial prostatitis
  -- Inpatient: ORZID2 NE GMENU ABX CHRONIC BACT PROSTATITIS (matched by name)
     [both replace clinical]
       - Chronic bacterial prostatitis usually presents as recurrent urinary tract
       - infections with the same bacterial etiology. Pain, tenderness, and fever may
       - accompany intermittent episodes of cystitis, but men are usually asymptomatic
       - between episodes and the prostate examination may be normal. Non-steroidal anti-
       - inflammatory drugs (NSAIDs) often relieve pain. If the diagnosis is uncertain or
       - if the patient's symptoms do not respond to therapy, consult urology. The
       - presence of hematuria may be a sign of carcinoma, especially in older men, and a
       - urology consult should be requested.
       + Gram-negative rods are the most common etiologic agents, with Escherichia coli
       + causing approximately 75 to 80 percent of episodes. Enterococcus faecalis,
       + Klebsiella pneumoniae, Proteus mirabilis, Pseudomonas aeruginosa, and other
       + gram-negative bacilli are the next most commonly reported organisms.
       + Staphylococcus aureus and streptococcal species are occasional pathogens.
       + Chronic bacterial prostatitis is often presumptively diagnosed and empirically
       + treated with antimicrobials when men present with chronic (eg, longer than three
       + months) or recurrent urogenital symptoms, particularly if bacteriuria is also
       + present. Pain, tenderness, and fever may accompany intermittent episodes of
       + cystitis, but men are usually asymptomatic between episodes and the prostate
       + examination may be normal. Non-steroidal anti- inflammatory drugs (NSAIDs) often
       + relieve pain. Referral to an urologist can be helpful for men with long-standing
       + or refractory prostatic symptoms to obtain expressed prostatic fluid for
       + diagnosis.
     [both replace]
       - Chronic prostate pain syndrome should be considered in patients who have
       - symptoms of prostatitis but have negative urine cultures. Chronic prostate pain
       - syndrome is discussed elsewhere:
       - Chronic prostate pain syndrome
       + Lab Menu
     [both replace clinical]
       - 4 weeks minimum and up to 6 weeks of treatment is required. Durations longer
       - than 6 weeks may be needed in treatment of resistant organisms. Treatment with
       - trimethoprim/sulfamethoxazole requires a treatment duration of 1 to 3 months.
       + At least 4 to 6 weeks of treatment is required. Durations longer than 6 weeks ma
     [both replace clinical]
       - Preferred:
       - Levofloxacin 750 mg PO q24h ($) [R,DI] for 4 weeks
       + 1st line:
       + Ciprofloxacin 500 mg PO q12h ($) [R,DI]
     [both replace clinical]
       - Levofloxacin 750 mg IV q24h ($) [R,DI] for 4 weeks
       - Alternative:

     [both replace clinical]
       - Chronic bacterial prostatitis usually presents as recurrent urinary tract
       - infections with the same bacterial etiology. Pain, tenderness, and fever may
       - accompany intermittent episodes of cystitis, but men are usually asymptomatic
       - between episodes and the prostate examination may be normal. Non-steroidal anti-
       - inflammatory drugs (NSAIDs) often relieve pain. If the diagnosis is uncertain or
       - if the patient's symptoms do not respond to therapy, consult urology. The
       - presence of hematuria may be a sign of carcinoma, especially in older men, and a
       - urology consult should be requested.
       + Gram-negative rods are the most common etiologic agents, with Escherichia coli
       + causing approximately 75 to 80 percent of episodes. Enterococcus faecalis,
       + Klebsiella pneumoniae, Proteus mirabilis, Pseudomonas aeruginosa, and other
       + gram-negative bacilli are the next most commonly reported organisms.
       + Staphylococcus aureus and streptococcal species are occasional pathogens.
       + Chronic bacterial prostatitis is often presumptively diagnosed and empirically
       + treated with antimicrobials when men present with chronic (eg, longer than three
       + months) or recurrent urogenital symptoms, particularly if bacteriuria is also
       + present. Pain, tenderness, and fever may accompany intermittent episodes of
       + cystitis, but men are usually asymptomatic between episodes and the prostate
       + examination may be normal. Non-steroidal anti- inflammatory drugs (NSAIDs) often
       + relieve pain. Referral to an urologist can be helpful for men with long-standing
       + or refractory prostatic symptoms to obtain expressed prostatic fluid for
       + diagnosis.

=== cholecystitis [local-clinical, auto] Cholecystitis (Navigation)
  -- Inpatient: ORZID2 NE GMENU ABX CHOLECYSTITIS (matched by name)
     [both delete clinical]
       - For acute cholecystitis in grade 1 disease, antimicrobials are primarily
       - prophylactic. For moderate to severe disease grades 2-3, antimicrobials should
       - be continued for 4-7 days.
     [both replace]
       - Enterobacter. Anaerobic coverage is not indicated unless a biliary-enteric
       - abnormality is present. For community-acquired acute cholecystitis, enterococcal
       - coverage is not required because Enterococcus has not been shown to be
       - pathogenic in these patients

=== comm-acq-diarrhea [local-clinical, auto] Community acquired diarrhea (no recent travel)
  -- Inpatient: ORZID2 NE GMENU COMM ACQ DIARRHEA (matched by name)
     [both replace clinical]
       - Consider Clostridioides difficile infection:
       - Clostridioides difficile treatment recommendations
       + Most episodes of acute diarrhea are self-limited. In most mild to moderate
       + cases, antimicrobial therapy does not provide benefit.
       + Acute diarrhea (< 14 days of symptoms)
       + Empirical antimicrobials should be given only in cases of severe acute disease
       + or high-risk conditions: fever, >/= 6 stools in 24 hours, volume depletion
       + requiring hospitalization, severe abdominal pain, immune compromise, cardiac
       + disease, >/= 70 years old.
       + If in the preceding two months the patient has taken a PPI or has been
       + hospitalized for three or more days, consider Clostridium difficile infection.
       + Clostridium difficile
       + Bloody diarrhea
       + Bloody diarrhea can represent hemorrhagic colitis or dysentery. Antimicrobial
       + therapy and antimotility agents are not recommended in most patients with bloody
       + diarrhea due to an increased risk of hemolytic uremic syndrome when using these
       + agents. Obtain a stool sample for enteric pathogen PCR testing and reserve
       + antimicrobial and antimotility agent treatment until results are known. For more
       + information on when an antimicrobial is indicated or if the patient has
       + severe/life threatening diarrhea or is immunocompromised, contact Infectious
       + Diseases.
     [both replace]
       - Empiric antimicrobial treatment
       + Empiric antimicrobial treatment (for severe disease or risk of severe disease)

=== flu-seasonal-influenza [local-clinical, partial] Influenza vaccination recommendations
  -- Inpatient: ORZID2 NE GMENU ABX INFLUENZA VACCINATION (matched by name)
     [one replace clinical dated]
       - INFLUENZA VACCINATION RECOMMENDATIONS
       - Influenza Vaccine Outpatient and Inpatient Guidelines
       - Annual influenza vaccination is recommended for all persons aged 6 months and
       - older without a specific contraindication.
       - Eggs and latex allergies are NOT considered contraindications to receiving the
       - vaccine, the only contraindication to the flu vaccination is severe allergic
       - reaction (i.e. Anaphylactic reactions).
       - Recommend observing all patients for 15 minutes after receiving any influenza
       - vaccine. A previous severe allergic reaction to influenza vaccine, regardless of
       - the component suspected of being responsible for the reaction, is a
       - contraindication to future receipt of ANY influenza vaccine.
       - Outpatient (excluding ED) Influenza Vaccination Recommendations
       - #For age 65 or older administer Fluad Adjuvanted Trivalent 0.5ml syringe.
       - #For age 64 or under administer Afluria Trivalent 0.5ml syringe.
       + NWIHCS Vaccination Recommendations for Inpatients and Outpatients
       + Influenza Vaccine 2023-2024:
       + *Annual influenza vaccination is recommended for all persons aged 6
       + months and older without a specific contraindication.
       + *Quadrivalent influenza vaccine is made up of the four flu strains most likely
       + to cause the flu during the upcoming season.
       + *Quadrivalent vaccine contains two influenza A strains (H1N1 and H3N2) and
       + two influenza B viruses.
       + *Fluzone HIGH Dose Quadrivalent vaccine contains a four-fold higher antigen
       + content per dose than standard dose influenza vaccines to help create a
       + stronger immune response.
       + Eggs and latex allergies are NOT considered contraindications to receiving
       + the vaccine, the only contraindication to the flu vaccination is severe
       + allergic reaction (i.e., Anaphylactic reactions).
       + Recommend observing all patients for 15 minutes after receipt of any
       + influenza vaccine. A previous severe allergic reaction to influenza vaccine,
       + regardless of the component suspected of being responsible for the reaction,
       + is a contraindication to future receipt of ANY influenza vaccine.
       + Influenza 2023-2024 Vaccination Recommendations:
       + Outpatient Influenza Vaccine:
       + *For age 65 or older administer Fluzone HIGH DOSE Quadrivalent 0.7ml syringe.
       + *For age 64 or under administer Fluzone Quadrivalent 0.5ml syringe.
     [both delete clinical shared:636A6-DesMoines,656-StCloud]
       - For those who do not trigger clinical reminder, order below:
       - Influenza once dose for outpatients that do not trigger clinical reminder
     [both replace clinical]
       - Inpatient and ED Vaccination Recommendations
       - Influenza orders are part of the admission order set. For those who require an
       - order due to admission order being discontinued/expired or if patient was
       - admitted prior to influenza season, use the order below:
       - [Click Here] Influenza Vaccination Inpatient and ED 25/26

       - #For age 64 or under administer Afluria Trivalent 0.5ml syringe.

=== prev-of-bact-endocarditis [local-clinical, fallback] Prevention of bacterial endocarditis
  -- Inpatient: ORZID2 NE GMENU PREV OF BACT ENDOCARDITIS (matched by name)
     [both replace shared:656-StCloud]
       - Bacteremia occurs several times a day during daily activities like eating and
       - brushing teeth and rarely results in endocarditis, even in people with abnormal
       - valves. While bacteremia might occur following dental or other procedures, most
       - cases of endocarditis are from the millions more episodes of transient
       - bacteremia a person experiences per year from ordinary daily activities. There
       - is no evidence that antimicrobial prophylaxis prevents endocarditis after
       - procedures. The best available evidence suggests that prophylactic
       - antimicrobials confer more risks than benefits in most situations. A decision
       - about whether to administer antimicrobial prophylaxis should be based on both
       - the risk of endocarditis conferred by the patient's cardiac defect and the risk
       - of bacteremia from a planned dental or surgical procedure.
       - Minneapolis VAHCS SOP antimicrobial prophylaxis for dental surgery:
       - SOP for dental surgery antimicrobial prophylaxis

       - For more information contact Infectious Diseases.

       + Prosthetic joints have a small risk (<1 to 5% for the time the prosthesis is in
       + place) of infection from hematogenous seeding. The risk of joint infection
       + after dental or surgical procedures is exceedingly small. Existing data do not
       + support the use of prophylactic antimicrobial prophylaxis for patients with
       + prosthetic joints undergoing procedures. Since the overall risk of prosthesis
       + infection is slightly higher during the first 2 years after implantation, some
       + physicians give prophylactic antimicrobials for patients during the first 2
       + years after implantation. In addition, antimicrobials are given for theoretical
       + reasons to persons with neutropenia (less than 500 neutrophils and bands per
       + milliliter).
       + If antimicrobials are given during dental procedures to prevent prosthetic joint
       + infection, give the following:
       + Preferred
       + Amoxicillin 2 gm PO 1 hour before procedure ($0.56/day) [R] < OR >
       + Cephalexin 2 gm PO 1 hour before procedure ($0.36/day) [R]
       + Alternative for patients with penicillin allergy
       + Clindamycin 600 mg PO 30 minutes before procedure ($1.68/day) [H,O]
       + Patients unable to take oral medications
       + Ampicillin 2 gm IV over 20 minutes, beginning 60 minutes before incision
       + ($12/day) [R] < OR >
       + Cefazolin 1 gm IV over 20 minutes, beginning 60 minutes before incision
       + ($2.73/day) [R]
       + Alternative for patients with penicillin allergy
       + Clindamycin 600 mg IV over 30 minutes, beginning 60 minutes before incision
       + ($1.44/day) [H,O]
     [one replace clinical dated shared:656-StCloud]

=== prevention-of-prosthetic-joint-infection-with-dirty-operation-site [local-clinical, partial] Prevention of prosthetic joint infection before infected skin, skin structures or muskuloskeletal tissue procedures
  -- Inpatient: ORZID2 NE GMENU ABX DERM IE JI PROPHYLAXIS (matched by name)
     [one replace clinical shared:636A6-DesMoines]
       - PREVENTION OF PROSTHETIC JOINT INFECTION BEFORE INFECTED SKIN, SKIN STRUCTURES
       - OR MUSKULOSKELETAL TISSUE PROCEDURES
       - There is a lack of evidence supporting antimicrobial prophylaxis to prevent
       - prosthetic joint infections in patients undergoing procedures on infected skin,
       - skin structures or musculoskeletal tissue procedures.
       - For more information contact Infectious Diseases.
       + INFECTED SKIN SURGICAL SITE PROSTHETIC JOINT INFECTION PROPHYLAXIS
       + Antimicrobial prophylaxis is indicated in procedures involving infected skin
       + skin structures, or infected musculoskeletal tissue when the patient is at
       + high risk of hematogenous prosthetic joint infection.
       + Hematogenous prosthetic joint infection high risk conditions:
       + 1. First two years following joint replacement
       + 2. Previous prosthetic joint infection
       + 3. Immunocompromised
       + 4. Insulin dependent diabetes
       + 5. HIV infection
       + 6. Malignancy
       + 7. Malnutrition
       + 8. Hemophilia
       + If patient has one or more of the above conditions, antimicrobial prophylaxis is
       + indicated. Use the following link for antimicrobial prophylaxis recommendations.
       + Antimicrobial Prophylaxis in Patients with Prosthetic Joints

=== hidradenitis-suppurativa [local-clinical, fallback] Hidradenitis suppurativa (Navigation)
  -- Inpatient: ORZID2 NE GMENU HIDRADENITIS SUPPURATIVA (matched by name)
     [one replace clinical shared:636A6-DesMoines,656-StCloud]
       - A chronic inflammatory condition that typically involves occlusion, rupture,
       - abscess formation and infection of hair follicles as well as secondary infection
       - of apocrine glands/surrounding tissues. Lesions most often occur in the
       - intertriginous areas (axillae, inguinal and anogenital) in is associated with
       - smoking and obesity. There is a 40% genetic predisposition for hidradenitis
       - suppurativa (HS) and is often accompanied by pain, malodor, and drainage.
       - Cultures are negative 50% of the time and often are polymicrobial with S.
       - epidermidis and S. aureus most isolated. The Hurley Staging System is often used
       - to determine severity of HS.
       - Therapy generally consists of treatment of comorbidities that can exacerbate HS,
       - avoidance of triggers, pain management, wound care, and topical/oral
       - antimicrobials. Hormonal agents, steroids, retinoids, and surgery can also be
       - used to treat certain patients. Biologics may be considered in Stage II-III HS.
       - Consult Dermatology in all cases
       - Hurley Staging System:
       - Stage I: recurrent nodules and abscesses with minimal scarring
       - Stage II: limited number of sinuses and/or scarring within a body region
       - Stage III: multiple or extensive sinuses and/or scarring
       - Duration of Therapy:
       - Treatment is typically for 12 weeks but may require longer durations in certain
       - cases. Metronidazole should only be used for a maximum of 6 weeks to avoid
       - neurologic toxicity.
       - Interventions for All Patients:
       - Smoking cessation, weight loss, avoidance of triggers, and pain management
       - should be part of all treatment plans. Consult Dermatology for use of hormonal
       - agents, steroids, retinoids, and surgery.
       - Optional Topical Treatment, May be Used in Addition to Oral Therapies Below:
       - Chlorhexidine gluconate 4% liquid topically wash affected area q24h ($) [M]
       - < OR >
       - Benzoyl peroxide 10% soap topically wash affected area q24h ($) [M]
       - < ADD ON THERAPY FOR FLARES - MUST USE IN ADDITION TO ONE OF THE AGENTS ABOVE>
       - Clindamycin 1% solution topically to affected areas q12h prn for flares ($) [M]
       - Stage I-II Initial Treatment:
       - Doxycycline 100 mg PO q12h ($) [DI] for 12 weeks
       - < OR >
       - Minocycline 100 mg PO q12h ($) [DI] for 12 weeks
       - Stage III Initial Treatment and Stage I-II Failure of Oral Doxycycline:
       - Clindamycin 300 mg PO q12h for 12 weeks ($) [H,O]
       - Stage II-III Failure of Oral Clindamycin:
       - Moxifloxacin 400 mg PO q24h ($)[R,DI] for 12 weeks <AND> metronidazole 500
       - mg q8h ($) [DI] for 6 weeks <AND> rifampin 300 mg PO q12h ($) [DI,O] for 12
       - weeks
       - _______________________________________________________________________________
       + Hidradenitis suppurativa results from occlusion of hair follicles and the
       + typical lesion is a pea-sized cyst. Symptoms include pruritus, burning,
       + erythema, and local hyperhidrosis.
       + Treat with topical antimicrobials. For severe disease, or if topical
       + antimicrobials are not effective, consult dermatology. Surgical excision may
       + be necessary for extensive disease.
       + Treatment
       + Treat for 3 months
       + Clindamycin 1% topical solution apply twice daily ($) [M]
     [none replace clinical shared:636A6-DesMoines,656-StCloud]
       - Alikhan, Ali, et al. Journal of the American Academy of Dermatology 81.1 (2019):
       - 76-90
       - John Hopkins ABX Guide Keyword: Hidradenitis Suppurativa
       - Up-To-Date article: Hidradenitis suppurativa: Management
       - Up-To-Date article: Hidradenitis suppurativa: Pathogenesis, clinical features,
       - and diagnosis
       + Up-to-date website
  -- Outpatient: ORZID3 NE GMENU HIDRADENITIS SUPPURATIVA (matched by name)

=== ophthalmic-surgery-prophylaxis [local-clinical, fallback] Ophthalmic surgery prophylaxis
  -- Inpatient: ORZID2 NE GMENU SURG OPHTHALMIC SURG (matched by name)
     [none replace clinical]
       - When indicated, ophthalmology will order antimicrobial prophylaxis before
       - procedures.
       - Cataract Surgery
       - Intracameral moxifloxacin is recommended for use to reduce the risk of
       - post-operative endophthalmitis after cataract surgery only in those patients
       - at highest risk of infection, including the following:
       - * Patients with prior intravitreal injections
       - * Immunosuppressed patients
       - * Patients with significant ocular surface eyelid disease
       - * Complication occurs during surgery (i.e. capusle rupture, vitreous loss)
       - * Other possible high risk situations as they arise but not listed above
       - Preferred:
       - Moxifloxacin 500mcg/0.5ml Eye Inj Intracameral Cataract (Conc.=100mcg/0.1ml)
       - References:
       - Arshinoff S., J Cataract Refract Surg, (2016) 42:1730-1741
       + Polymyxin/trimethoprim ophthalmic drops: 1 drop both eyes four times a day
       + for 2 days prior to surgery, then continue for 2 weeks after surgery.
       + Ciprofloxacin 0.3 %: 1 drop both eyes four times a day for 2 days prior to
       + surgery then continue for 2 weeks after surgery.
       + Moxifloxacin 0.5 %: 1 drop both eyes four times a day for 2 days prior to
       + surgery, then continue for 2 weeks after surgery.
       + Erythromycin 0.5 % ophthalmic ointment: apply thin film to both eyes qid for 2
       + days prior to surgery, then continue for 2 weeks after surgery.
  -- Outpatient: ORZID2 NE GMENU SURG OPHTHALMIC SURG (matched by name)
     [none replace clinical]

=== surg-timing-dosing-prophyl-abx [local-clinical, partial] surg-timing-dosing-prophyl-abx
  -- Inpatient: ORZID2 NE GMENU SURG TIMING DOSING PROPHYL ABX (matched by name)
     [both replace clinical]
       - For optimal prevention, antimicrobial concentrations should be above the minimal
       - inhibitory concentration (MIC) of potential pathogens from initial incision
       - until closure.
       - There is no advantage to giving post-operative doses. Despite a lack of
       - demonstrated benefit, post-operative doses are sometimes given in clinical
       - practice. If post-operative doses are given, they should be discontinued by 24
       - hours after closure of the incision.
       - When prophylaxis consists of a single dose as should be the case in the majority
       - of procedures, adjustment for renal or hepatic dysfunction is not necessary.
       - The optimal time for administration of preoperative doses is 30 to
       - 60 minutes before surgical incision for most antimicrobials.
       - Infusions of drugs like fluoroquinolones and vancomycin that require longer
       - infusion times should begin 60 to 120 minutes before first incision.
       - During procedures that last so long that concentrations might fall below the
       - MICs of potential pathogens, repeated doses of antimicrobials should be given.
       - Re-dosing may also be warranted if prolonged or excessive bleeding
       - occurs.
       - Doses and Redosing Recommendations for Common Antimicrobials
       + Parenteral prophylactic antimicrobials can be given as a single IV dose begun
       + within 60 minutes before the initial incision. For procedures lasting > 2 half-
       + lives of the prophylactic agent (generally surgeries over 4 hours or those with
       + major blood loss, intraoperative supplementary doses may be required. The timing
       + to redosing common antibiotics in patients with normal renal function are:
       + Ampicillin/sulbactam- 2 hrs:
       + Cefoxitin - 2 hrs:
       + Cefazolin - 4 hrs:
       + Clindamycin- 6 hrs:
       + Metronidazole - 8 hrs,
       + If vancomycin or a fluoroquinolone is used, the infusion should start 60-120
       + minutes prior to the initial incision to have adequate tissue levels and to
       + minimize the possibility of an infusion reaction close to the time of induction
       + of anesthesia.
       + In patients that are colonized with methicillin-resistant Staphylococcus aureus
       + MRSA) or in institutions where surgical site infections are frequently due to
       + MRSA, vancomycin could be considered as prophylaxis. It should be given in
       + addition to the routine prophylactic regimen recommended for the procedure.
       + Vancomycin is less effective than cefazolin for prevention of infections due to
       + methicillin-susceptible Staphylococcus aureus (MSSA).
       + Preoperative Screening and Decolonization-Preoperative identification of
       + patients who are nasal carrier of MRSA or MSSA and decolonization using
       + intranasal mupirocin and chlorhexidine have been shown to decrease surgical site
       + infections following some procedures (primarily cardiac and orthopedic), but
       + resistance to mupirocin could emerge if it is used routinely.
     [one replace clinical dated]
       - in surgery, Am J Health-Syst Pharm. 2013 Vol 70 page 195-283
       - Antimicrobial Prophylaxis for Surgery, Treatment Guidelines from The
       - Medical Letter 2012 Vol 10 pages 73-77
       + in surgery, Am J Health-Syst Pharm. 2013: 70: 195-283
       + Antimicrobial Prophylaxis for Surgery, Treatment Guidelines from The Medical
       + Letter 2016
  -- Outpatient: ORZID2 NE GMENU SURG TIMING DOSING PROPHYL ABX (matched by name)

=== surgical-prophylaxis-general-information [local-clinical, partial] Surgical prophylaxis general information
  -- Inpatient: ORZID2 NE GMENU SURG PROPHYL GENRL INFO (matched by name)
     [both replace clinical]
       - 4. administered for the shortest effective period to minimize adverse
       - effects, the development of resistance, and costs.
       + 4) administered for the shortest effective period to minimize adverse effects,
       + the development of resistance, and costs.
       + Most experts do not recommend routine use of broad-spectrum antibiotics such as
       + ertapenem or extended spectrum cephalosporins such as cefotaxime, ceftriaxone,
       + ceftazidime, cefepime or ceftaroline for surgical prophylaxis because they are
       + expensive, some less active against staphylococci than first or second
       + generation cephalosporins, and their spectrum of activity often includes
       + organisms rarely encountered in elective surgery.
     [one replace clinical dated]
       - Bratzler DW, et al. Clinical practice guidelines for antimicrobial prophylaxis

       - Antimicrobial Prophylaxis for Surgery, Treatment Guidelines from The
       - Medical Letter 2012 Vol 10 pages 73-77
       + Antimicrobial Prophylaxis for Surgery, Medical Letter: May 23, 2016
       + Sanford Guidelines online

=== aspergillus-infections-general [local-clinical, fallback] Aspergillus species
  -- Inpatient: ORZID2 NE GMENU FUNGI ASPERGILLUS SPP. (matched by name)
     [both replace clinical shared:636A6-DesMoines,656-StCloud]
       - Amphotericin B Liposomal 3-5MG/KG IV q24h ($$$$)[R,O]+ NS Hydratio
       + Liposomal amphotericin (Ambisome) 3-5 mg/kg IV q24h ($$$$) [R,O]
  -- Outpatient: ORZID3 NE GMENU FUNGI ASPERGILLUS SPP. (matched by name)
     [none replace clinical shared:636A6-DesMoines,656-StCloud]
       - -Aspergillus species are ubiquitous, but invasive aspergillosis occurs primarily
       - in immunocompromised hosts. Neutropenia and glucocorticoid use are the most
       - common predisposing factors. Optimal management involves early diagnosis and
       - early initiation of antifungal treatment.
       - -The most common clinical manifestation of invasive aspergillosis is pulmonary
       - disease, but other manifestations include central nervous system infection,
       - rhinosinusitis, endocarditis, gastrointestinal disease, annd others.
       - Duration of antimicrobial therapy:
       - Duration of therapy should be a minimum of 6 to 12 weeks, although duration
       - is highly dependent on degree/duration of immunosuppression, disease site, and
       - and evidence of disease improvement. Consult Infectious Disease.
       - Severe disease:
       - Refer to Inpatient CDSS for treatment.
       - Mild/moderate disease:
       - Preferred treatment:
       - Voriconazole 400 mg PO BID for 2 doses, then 200 mg PO BID ($$) [R,H,DI]
       - Non-VA order for above
       - Alternative treatment:
       - Consult Infectious Disease for alternative.
       - References:
       - Patterson TF, et al. Clin Infect Dis. (2016) 63(4):433-442
       - UpToDate: Treatment and prevention of invasive aspergillosis
       + Refer to Inpatient Antimicrobial CDSS.

=== fever-of-unk-origin [local-clinical, partial] Fever of unknown origin
  -- Inpatient: ORZID2 NE GMENU FEVER OF UNK ORIGIN (matched by name)
     [one replace clinical]
       - week of hospital evaluation. Cases with FUO are often classified in four
       - categories: (1) classical, (2) neutropenic, (3) nosocomial, and (4) fever in
       - persons with HIV/AIDS. Empirical antimicrobial therapy is rarely indicated in
       - patients with FUO.
       - Note that FUO in neutropenic patients refers to persistent fever that has
       - remained undiagnosed after extensive diagnostic work and empirical antimicrobial
       - therapy. New fever in patients with profound neutropenia is a different syndrome
       - and does require empirical antimicrobial therapy. Infectious Diseases should be
       - involved in any case where empirical treatment for fever of unknown origin is
       - being considered. For more information refer to the neutropenic fever
       + week of hospital evaluation. Empirical antimicrobial therapy is rarely indicated
       + except in patients who are neutropenic, immunocompromised, or critically ill.
       + For prolonged fever in neutropenic patients refer to the neutropenic fever

=== leukocytosis [local-clinical, partial] Leukocytosis
  -- Inpatient: ORZID2 NE GMENU LEUKOCYTOSIS (matched by name)
     [one replace clinical]
       - The majority of leukocytosis cases are due to infection, but a sizable minority
       - are associated with physiological stress, inflammation, medications (e.g.
       - glucocorticoids), and hematological conditions. In a review of 100 cases of
       - extreme leukocytosis (more than 25,000 leukocytes per microliter with more than
       - 50% granulocytes) at the Minneapolis VA, only 48 were from infectious problems.
       - Infection should be considered as a possible stimulus for leukocytosis in a
       - patient with leukocytosis, especially when total white blood cell count is
       - greater than or equal to 25,000 per micro liter. Antimicrobials should be
       - administered if the patient has a syndrome or disease that calls for
       - antimicrobials. Refer to those recommendations for treatment. Antimicrobials
       - should not be administered solely to treat a patient with leukocytosis and no
       - other sign or symptom of infection.
       + Most leukocytosis cases are due to infection, but may also be
       + associated with physiological stress, inflammation, medications (e.g.
       + glucocorticoids), and hematological conditions.
       + Antimicrobials should be administered if the patient has a syndrome or disease
       + that calls for antimicrobials. Refer to those recommendations for treatment.
       + Antimicrobials should not be administered solely to treat a patient with
       + leukocytosis and no other sign or symptom of infection.
  -- Outpatient: ORZID2 NE GMENU LEUKOCYTOSIS (matched by name)

=== streptococci [local-clinical, auto] Bacteremia associated with streptococci
  -- Inpatient: ORZID2 NE GMENU BACTEREMIA STREPTOCOCCI (matched by name)

       - Bacteremia is typically caused by S. pyogenes (Group A), S. agalactiae (Group B)
       - and S. pneumoniae. If other streptococcal species are isolated contamination is
       - likely. Treat only if greater than one blood culture is positive. Identification
       - and treatment of the source of infection, in addition to antimicrobial therapy,
       - is crucial for resolution of infection.
       - For patients with toxic shock syndrome refer to those recommendations.
       - Toxic Shock Syndrome

       + Treatment of S. Pneumoniae or other streptococcal isolates

       - Assessment of beta-lactam reaction [CLICK HERE]
